The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 SECTION 1.0
 INTRODUCTION

1.1 Background

 Databases of high-level waste (HLW) and low activity waste (LAW) glass properties and
compositions have been collected at the Vitreous State Laboratory (VSL) to support the
vitrification processes at the Hanford Tank Waste Treatment and Immobilization Plant (WTP).
The databases are used to develop HLW and LAW property-composition models that are
intended to control the vitrification processes to ensure that WTP glasses will meet their
respective product quality and processability requirements. The quality and the validity ranges of
the property-composition models will greatly influence waste treatment operations, including the
waste loading that is achievable for each waste composition. The current baseline models for
WTP operation were developed by VSL under the Bechtel National, Inc. (BNI) WTP contract [1-
4]. These models were based on HLW and LAW glass property data collected over a range of
compositions that was guided primarily by the WTP contract and the specific set of tanks
specified therein.

 Since the development of the baseline WTP models, the Office of River Protection (ORP)
has directed a program of work at VSL to examine various approaches for optimization of the
performance of the WTP, including increasing the waste loadings in HLW glasses [5–21]. As
more property-composition data and better information on HLW compositions became available,
part of the program at VSL involved the update, expansion, and enhancement of the HLW
property-composition models. As was the case with the WTP baseline models development,
expansion and enhancement of the various HLW property-composition models is a multi-year
effort that employs a phased approach. A compilation of the ORP glass data and results from
Phase 1 and Phase 2 of models enhancements have been reported previously [22-24]. This report
presents and discusses the data collected during Phase 3 of enhancement work and the updated
property-composition models developed for Hanford HLW glasses.

 The WTP baseline HLW models were developed to predict the following glass
properties: Product Consistency Test (PCT) responses for B-, Li-, and Na-releases, spinel
crystallization (one-percent crystal fraction temperature, T1%), melt viscosity, melt electrical
conductivity (EC), and Toxicity Characteristic Leach Procedure (TCLP) Cd responses [1, 2]. No
baseline WTP models were developed to control nepheline formation upon slow cooling (i.e.,
Canister Center Cooling [CCC]). During earlier Phase 1 ORP work new property-composition
data were developed and incorporated, expanding the number of HLW glasses in the database
from 305 to over 400; the WTP baseline models were also updated using the expanded dataset
during Phase 1 [22, 23]. In addition, Phase 1 testing included collection of data on nepheline
formation upon CCC. Development of models to predict nepheline formation, however, is the
subject of a separate ORP task. Phase 1 models were developed for the expanded composition
region including the higher waste loading ORP glass formulations. The regression and validation

 12

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

statistics for the Phase 1 models are comparable to or slightly worse than those for the original
WTP baseline models but the new models are valid over much wider composition ranges. The
Phase 1 viscosity and electrical conductivity models showed comparable or slightly better
performance when compared to the respective WTP baseline models, in spite of a greatly
expanded composition region [23].

 The WTP and ORP Phase 1 models are “global” models in the sense that each model uses
a single model form over the entire glass composition region of interest. A potential
disadvantage of such models is that their performance may worsen as the compositional spaces
expand, yielding less accurate property prediction performances. In such cases, it may be useful
to consider “local” models, which may be more accurate since they cover smaller compositional
regions. Preliminary local models were developed in Phase 1 for sub-regions of low alumina
(Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and high alumina (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) [23]. This choice of division was
made over other choices because: (i) while the WTP glasses were developed primarily for iron-
limited wastes, many of the ORP glasses were formulated specifically for the aluminum- and the
aluminum/sodium-limited wastes; (ii) the maximum component limit for Al2O3 in the WTP
baseline model validity region is 13.1 wt%; and (iii) the design feature for the Phase 1 glass test
matrix (high-alumina augmentation) limits the Al2O3 content to a minimum of 13.1 wt%. The
Phase 1 low-alumina local models generally showed better performance than the global models
[23]. This was expected as there were sizable overlaps in the data used in developing both kinds
of models. Development of high-alumina models, by contrast, was limited by the paucity of
available data. Not only is the high alumina region covered by fewer glasses but, in addition,
most of the high-alumina glasses were actively designed and therefore provide relatively uneven
coverage.

 Following the Phase 1 approach, Phase 2 work incorporated HLW glass data collected for
other ORP tasks; all of those glasses were actively designed [24]. Phase 2 work also included the
statistical design of two HLW glass matrices to augment and improve the coverage in the high
alumina region: one matrix with 23 glasses covered the alumina range of 13.1 to 22.1 wt% Al2O3
and another with 22 glasses covered the compositional space of ≥ 22.1 wt% Al2O3. The HLW
glass property-composition models were revised using the expanded Phase 2 dataset, which
comprised 494 glasses. The revised global models from Phase 2 for predicting EC and viscosity
showed similar or better performance when compared to the respective Phase 1 and WTP
baseline models, even though they are applicable to much wider compositional ranges. Local
models were not considered for EC and viscosity in Phase 2 because of the Phase 1 findings that
global model performances for these properties are comparable to or better than the WTP
baseline models, suggesting that local models may not offer significant advantages over the
global models in these cases. Global models for the other properties performed very similarly to
both the WTP baseline models and the Phase 1 global models. The same conclusion was drawn
for the low-alumina local (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) models. The local models for the high-alumina
region, on the other hand, benefited from addition of new data and produced improved modeling
and validation statistics.

 The present Phase 3 work continues the model development efforts of Phase 1 and Phase
2. It also follows a similar approach. Specifically, HLW glass property-composition data from
other ORP tasks conducted since the completion of Phase 2 were compiled and incorporated into

 13

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

the database. After a review of the combined WTP/ORP database, statistical design was used to
produce two HLW glass matrices to augment the identified compositional gaps. After ORP
review and approval of the test matrices, crucible melts were prepared and glass properties were
measured. The HLW glass property-composition models were then updated using the expanded
database.

1.2 Objectives

 Since the WTP compositional space of HLW glasses is extremely large, development of
HLW property-composition models is a multi-year task consisting of multiple phases with major
steps as discussed below. The focus is to extend and enhance the present WTP models of interest
including PCT releases, spinel crystallization (T1%), viscosity, electrical conductivity, TCLP-Cd
response, and nepheline formation on CCC heat treatment. Spinel crystallization and nepheline
formation on CCC are expected to be the most constraining with respect to waste loading. The
present work is a continuation of earlier development phases and is responsive to the applicable
Test Plan [25]. The objectives of this work, which are similar to those defined for the earlier
phases, were to:

 • Incorporate property-composition data from new glasses into the database.

 • Assess the expanded WTP-ORP database and HLW property-composition models.

 • Identify composition spaces in the database that need augmentation.

 • Develop statistically-designed composition matrices to cover the composition regions
 identified in the above analysis.

 • Prepare crucible melts of glass compositions from the statistically-designed composition
 matrices and measure the properties of interest.

 • Incorporate the above property-composition data of the matrix glasses into the database.

 • Assess HLW models with extended datasets.

 • Develop new enhanced HLW property-composition models.

 The models presented in this report will be revised as efforts continue to expand and
 improve the models based on additional data collection that is planned in future phases of this
 work.

 14

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 SECTION 2.0
 IDENTIFICATION OF COMPOSITION GAPS AND
 DEVELOPMENT OF AUGMENTATION MATRICES

 The dataset that supported the development of baseline WTP HLW property-composition
models includes all data collected under the WTP project since 1998 that satisfy all applicable
quality assurance (QA) requirements [2]. The dataset includes glasses that were actively
designed to treat specific HLW tank waste streams to meet all product quality and processing
requirements, with little or no intent to evenly cover a composition space. To supplement these
glass compositions, the WTP project also directed the testing of glass compositions that were
developed with statistical experimental design methods. A total of 5 HLW glass matrices were
designed, fabricated and characterized for augmentation of the actively designed WTP glasses.
These statistically designed glasses were intended to provide better coverage of the
compositional space of interest.

 Incorporation of new HLW glass data from the ORP test program also includes both
actively and statistically designed glasses. Since the conclusion of Phase 2 work to develop
enhanced HLW glass property-composition models [24], VSL has developed a range of glass
formulations for ORP to address specific test objectives. For example, new HLW glass
formulations were developed and tested to demonstrate the viability of vitrification of inorganic
ion-exchange media and to collect data for development of property-composition models for
properties not covered by the WTP baseline models. In addition to providing better coverage of
the existing HLW compositional space, the new ORP glasses continue to expand the
compositional ranges. An important element in this task was to assess the updated database to
identify compositional gaps and, based on this assessment, to design statistical matrices for
augmentation. This section provides a summary of the glass formulations developed since Phase
2 and describes the development of augmentation test matrices.

2.1 Incorporation of New Property-Composition Data

 Four HLW glass studies have been completed at VSL since the conclusion of the Phase 2
modeling work. Glasses from these studies were both actively and statistically designed to meet
the various test objectives. Not all of these glasses were included in the basis for the statistical
design of the test matrices in this work, either because they were not available in time or because
they were removed during initial screening. All glasses were included, however, in the
development of new models. This subsection summarizes the sources of the new data and the
objectives of the studies that provided the data. Table 2.1 lists these glass studies and the number
of glasses added into the database.

 15

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

Phase 2 Support for HLW Direct Feed [26]

 Glass formulations were developed for four waste blends from Hanford tank A-104 with
varying amounts of LAW and HLW. The four waste blends represented different pretreatments
of the A-104 HLW, ranging from no washing to fully washed HLW solids. While HLW glasses
could be formulated for all four waste blends with acceptable processing and product properties,
testing showed that spinel crystallization was the primary waste loading limiting constraint even
for the unwashed solids. Thus, the HLW components of the waste (Fe, Mn, Ni, and Cr), rather
than the LAW components (Na, S, and Cl), were waste loading limiting for all four waste blends.

 Fifteen HLW glass formulations were developed and tested for the four A-104 waste
blends on crucible scale. These glasses contained up to 16.55 wt% Fe2O3 and up to 20.51 wt%
Na2O. A series of melter tests were also performed on the DM100-BL vitrification system for the
four blends of simulated A-104 HLW solids and supernates with glass forming additives
optimized for each blend.

Calcium Phosphate Constraints in HLW Glasses: Phosphate Liquidus Model [27]

 This work focused on constructing a database of previously tested HLW glasses that
exhibited phosphate crystallization and augmenting the dataset with statistically designed glasses.
The statistical matrix consisted of two layers, with 10 new glasses in the inner layer and 15 in the
outer layer. The phosphate glass database, which included a total of 86 glasses, supported the
development of models for predicting phosphate liquidus temperature values based on glass
composition. Such models were not developed for the WTP baseline because high phosphorus
contents were not expected in the compositional ranges defined by the tanks specified in the
WTP contract (AZ-101, AZ-102, AY-102/C-106, and AY-101/C-104).

 The 25 matrix glasses spanned the compositional ranges of 0.63 wt% to 9.00 wt% P2O5
and 0.40 wt% to 8.94 wt% CaO. These can be compared with the respective maximum
component limits found in the WTP baseline database, which are 0.7 wt% P2O5 and 1.1 wt%
CaO [2].

Development of Nepheline Models for Hanford HLW Glass [28]

 As described in Section 1, no WTP baseline model was developed to control nepheline
crystallization upon CCC and the development of such a model is the subject of a separate ORP
task. Previous ORP work, including Phase 1 and Phase 2 of the present task, collected nepheline
crystallization data upon CCC. The collected data were augmented by the design of a statistical
matrix of 35 HLW glass formulations. The basis for augmentation included 83 glasses with high
alumina content (13.1 wt% to 22.1 wt%) and 67 glasses with ≥ 22.1 wt% Al2O3. Data collection
for the 35 matrix glasses centered on nepheline formation; only limited data were collected for
the properties of interest with respect to the WTP HLW baseline models.

 16

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 Three new models were developed using a database of 277 glasses, including the
WTP/ORP combined dataset. Different statistical analysis methods that are suited for categorical
data (e.g., yes/no) were employed to develop these models. The new models provided significant
improvements in misclassification rates of nepheline formation as well as low false negative
rates as compared to previous models.

Vitrification of Inorganic Ion-Exchange Media [29]

 Candidate inorganic ion-exchange media were evaluated in this work to determine their
effectiveness in removing cesium from a Hanford Envelope A LAW simulant. Selection of the
best performing ion-exchange media formed the basis for new HLW glass formulations to
demonstrate the vitrification of these media, both alone and in combination with WTP HLW
streams. The selected media were crystalline silicotitanate (CST, IONISV® R9120-B) and
synthetic chabazite (IONSIV® R9160-G). Glass formulations with relatively high waste loadings
were designed and tested to demonstrate that the spent media can be vitrified, either alone or in
combination with HLW streams, with little impact on melt rates while producing fully compliant
glass products. Test results also suggested that inclusion of CST into the Hanford HLW stream
will require additional investigation since considerable levels of Nb2O5 and TiO2 are introduced.
Specifically, important effects on spinel T1% are expected as significant amounts of TiO2 were
found in crystallized spinel phases. It is noted that the CST glasses contained up to > 3 wt%
Nb2O5 and > 5 wt % TiO2, compared with 0 wt% Nb2O5 and < 1.2 wt % TiO2 in the glass
compositional region for the WTP baseline models.

 As shown in Table 2.1, 92 HLW glasses from the glass formulation studies discussed
above are incorporated into the HLW databases, including 5 CCC samples that are considered
near replicates. Table 2.2 summarizes the compositions of these existing glasses. A summary of
the property data collected is given in Section 4.

2.2 Development of Augmentation Matrices

 During Phase 1 and Phase 2 of the enhancement efforts to augment the available WTP
and OPR glass database, 50 new HLW glasses from Phase 1 and 45 new HLW glasses from
Phase 2 were formulated and characterized; the results have been reported previously [23, 24].
Since the baseline WTP property-composition models and associated database are largely
centered on high iron HLW wastes, Phase 1 and Phase 2 of this work initiated a systematic effort
to expand the HLW property-composition database and models beyond the WTP HLW
composition space and, more specifically, to increase the data coverage in the high alumina
composition region and begin to bridge the gaps between the WTP data set and the higher waste
loading ORP data. The present work concentrated on further augmentation of existing HLW
glasses, in particular of those statistically designed in the previous phases, and involves the
design and characterization of 45 glasses planned for Phase 3. The additional data collected are
used to support further improvement to the property-composition models.

 17

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 To develop the test matrix, 190 existing WTP and ORP HLW glasses that had been
statistically designed previously were selected as the basis for the present design in order to
augment the coverage in the compositional space of interest. Amongst these gasses are 60 HLW
glasses developed for WTP projects (HLW02 and HLW03 series), and 130 HLW glasses
developed for ORP projects, including 50 HLW13 series glasses from Phase 1 of this work, 45
HLW14 series glasses from Phase 2, and 35 HLW15 series glasses developed to support
improvement of the nepheline discriminator [28]. Radioactive glasses were excluded from the
present statistical design. Upon inspection of the distribution of the 190 statistically designed
HLW glasses, two areas that have sparse data coverage were identified, which is particularly
visible with respect to Al2O3 content as, for example is shown in Figure 2.1. One of these low-
coverage regions is in the Ultra-High-Alumina (UHA) region ranging from 26 to 29 wt% Al2O3.
Another is in the Moderate-Alumina (MA) region ranging from 9 to 12 wt%, which is between
typical lower-Al ORP formulations and many high-Al WTP HLW formulations. In view of the
importance of the development of Al-limited HLW glass formulations and the potential benefit
of bridging the areas occupied by WTP HLW formulations and ORP HLW formulations, two
separate new HLW glass matrices (UHA matrix and MA matrix) were developed to fill the
aforementioned compositional gaps.

 The UHA matrix consists of two layers. The compositions of the UHA HLW glasses in
the Inner Layer and Outer Layer were developed using the commercial software package Design
Expert (V.8) [30] with a D-optimal mixture design. Table 2.3 lists the design parameters of 29
new HLW glasses in the UHA region, including several property constraints used to avoid
glasses with unreasonable values of properties such as melt viscosity, electrical conductivity,
spinel crystallization and PCT leaching. From the 17 glass components, 4 and 5 key components
were selected respectively as design variables in the Inner Layer and Outer Layer (see Table 2.3).
The 14 design points in the Inner Layer were calculated for a special cubic model in order to
include up to ternary interaction effects. The 15 design points in the Outer Layer, by comparison,
were calculated for a quadratic model in order to include up to quadratic interaction effects. The
two designs were calculated independently, giving a total of 29 glass formulations in the UHA
test matrix. Table 2.4 defines the grouped variables.

 A single-layer design was employed for the MA matrix. Similar to the design approach
used for the UHA region, 5 key components were chosen as variables for the design of the MA
matrix. Table 2.5 lists the compositional constraints and property constraints used. The 15 design
points were calculated for a quadratic model; the MA matrix also includes one replicate.

 Table 2.6 gives the compositions of the resulting UHA and MA matrix glasses, which are
designated as the HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA series respectively. The glasses
HLW16-MA10 and -MA11 constitute the replicate pair in the MA test matrix. Distribution of the
HLW16 glasses in comparison to the existing statistically designed HLW glasses is shown in a
scatter plot matrix in Figure 2.2. Selected binary plots of key glass components are shown in
more detail in Figure 2.3.

 18

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 SECTION 3.0
 EXPERIMENTAL METHODS

 The experimental procedures used in the preparation and characterization of the 45
HLW16 glasses discussed in Section 2.2 are presented in this section. The following subsections
describe the preparation of glass batches, crucible glass melting, glass composition analysis, and
test procedures for PCT, T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP, and nepheline formation
on canister centerline cooling (CCC).

3.1 Glass Batching and Preparation

 Glass preparation began with a batching sheet that provided information on the required
starting materials and their weights. The information included the chemicals needed,
identification of the chemicals according to vendors and catalog numbers with the associated
purity, and the amounts necessary to produce about 420 g of glass with the target composition.
Chemicals were weighed and batched according to the batching sheets. The information found in
the batching sheets, including actual weights of chemicals used and their associated purities, can
be used to calculate the composition of the glasses. The batching chemicals were determined
such that the calculated compositions equaled the target compositions of the HLW16 glasses.

 A blender was used to mix and homogenize the starting materials before they were
loaded into platinum-gold crucibles (Pt-Au) that were engraved with individual identification
numbers. The crucible melts were prepared in a random order. After batching was completed, the
loaded platinum-gold crucibles were placed inside a Deltech DT-28 (or DT-29) furnace with a
Eurotherm 2404 temperature controller. The glasses were melted for 120 minutes at 1150°C.
Mixing of the melt was accomplished mechanically using a platinum stirrer, beginning 15
minutes after the furnace temperature reached the target and continuing for the next 90 minutes.
The molten glass was poured at the end of the melting period onto a graphite plate to cool.

3.2 Analyses of Glass Compositions

 The primary method used for glass composition analysis was X-Ray Fluorescence (XRF)
spectroscopy on powdered glass samples. A PANalytical Axios mAX-Advanced wavelength
dispersive XRF spectrometer was used for this purpose. The XRF spectrometer was calibrated
over a range of glass compositions using standard reference materials traceable to the National
Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), as well as waste glasses such as Argonne
National Laboratory – Low Activity Waste Reference Material (ANL-LRM) [31] and Defense
Waste Processing Facility – Environmental Assessment (DWPF-EA) glass [32]. XRF analysis
provides data for most glass components of interest, except lithium and boron, which are

 19

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

analyzed for selected glasses by Direct Current Plasma - Atomic Emission Spectroscopy (DCP-
AES), as described below.

 Selected HLW glass samples were also analyzed by DCP-AES after being subjected to
microwave-assisted total acid dissolution in Teflon vessels according to VSL standard operating
procedures. Twenty milliliters of a 1:5 mixture of concentrated HF:HNO3 was diluted to 50 ml
and used for the dissolution. This procedure is similar to the ASTM Test Method C1412-99,
which also employs a mixture of concentrated HF and HNO3 in microwave digestion of
pulverized glass samples. However, supplemental use of HCl/H3BO3 is not included in the VSL
procedure because boron is normally one of the analytes. The resulting solutions were analyzed
by DCP-AES for all constituents except anionic species such as sulfur and halogens. These
results complement those obtained by XRF, specifically for boron and lithium, and confirm the
glass concentrations of components that exceed the present VSL upper evaluation limit for XRF.

3.3 Product Consistency Test

 The PCT was conducted using 4 g of crushed glass (100-200 mesh, 75-149 m) placed in
40 ml of test solution (de-ionized water) inside 304L stainless steel vessels. These test conditions
result in a ratio of the glass surface area to the solution volume of about 2000 m-1. The PCT tests
were performed at 90ºC for 7 days according to American Society of Testing and Materials
(ASTM) procedure [33], in accordance with the current WTP contract requirement [34]. All tests
were conducted in triplicate, in parallel with the DWPF-EA standard glass included in each test
set. The leachates were sampled after 7 days. One milliliter (ml) of sampled leachate was mixed
with 20 ml of 1M HNO3 and the resulting solution was analyzed by DCP-AES. Another 3 ml of
sampled leachate was used for pH measurement.

 In addition to the leachate concentrations, it is convenient and conventional to also
consider the normalized leachate concentrations. The normalization is performed by dividing the
concentration measured in the leachate for any given component by its fraction in the glass. Thus,
the normalized concentration C i of element i (in g/L) is calculated from the elemental
concentration ci measured in the leachate (in ppm) as:

 ci
 Ci  , (3.1)
 fi

where fi is the mass fraction of element i in the glass.

 The surface area of the glass sample tested and the volume of leachant used will also
affect the measured leachate concentrations and, therefore, a standard value of their ratio
(2000 m-1) is specified in the PCT method [33]. A further normalization for this effect is often
considered by dividing the normalized concentration by the ratio of the surface area of glass
exposed to the solution volume (S/V, in m-1). The normalized mass loss (in g/m2) is then
obtained from:

 20

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 Ci
 Li  , (3.2)
 (S /V )

where S/V is the ratio of the glass surface area to the volume of the leachant, which for the
standard PCT is nominally 2000 m-1. Assuming this value of S/V, if Ci is expressed in g/L, one
need only divide by two to obtain Li in g/m2. Specification 1 in the WTP contract [34] requires
that the normalized mass losses of PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na be below the respective values
for the DWPF-EA glass. The nominal values for normalized leachate concentrations from the
DWPF-EA glass [32] are 16.695, 9.565, and 13.346 g/L for PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na,
respectively.

3.4 One-Percent Crystal Fraction Temperature (T1%)

 Glass samples (about 3 to 5 grams each) were melted in a covered platinum or platinum
alloy crucible at a temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour to establish a common starting point before
heat treatment for 70 hours at prescribed temperatures between 750°C and 1150°C. At the end of
the heat-treatment period, the samples were quenched by contacting the crucible with cold water.
The sample was then prepared (grinding to –18 mesh) for Scanning Electron Microscopy/Energy
Dispersive X-ray Spectroscopy (SEM/EDS) to determine the volume fraction of crystalline phases
and for identification of the major crystalline phases. The SEM/EDS was performed using a
scanning electron microscope (JEOL Model JSM-5910LV equipped with Oxford Instruments
INCAEnergy 300 system). The crystal fraction (vol%) was generally obtained as the average of
four (up to eight) viewing area counts from the powdered samples. For each glass, a sufficient
number of heat treatments were performed to obtain non-zero crystallinity data for at least three
temperatures in order to reasonably constrain the T1% value. In a few cases, however, non-zero
crystallinity data were available for fewer than three temperatures. Effort was also made to bracket
the T1% value so that it could be estimated by interpolation rather than extrapolation.

 Alternatively, the heat-treated samples were analyzed by X-ray diffraction (XRD) using
whole pattern fitting. Powdered samples (–325 mesh) of heat-treated glasses were homogenized
with an internal standard (α-Al2O3) before XRD patterns were obtained with a Thermo-Scientific
X’tra θ-θ XRD system. Diffraction patterns were analyzed using JADE9 (MDI, Inc.) software with
the Rietveld whole pattern fitting module to identify the crystalline phase(s) and calculate the wt%
of each phase. The wt% crystallinity data were converted to vol% with the known densities of the
phases and the measured densities of the HLW glasses.

 The T1% value for each HLW glass was obtained by linear regression of the heat-treatment
temperature (°C) as the dependent variable versus crystal fraction (vol%) as the independent
variable. The choice of vol% as the independent variable, which has larger measurement errors than
temperature, is contrary to the selection that would normally be made for regression. As discussed
during the WTP models development [2], this “inverse regression” approach has the advantage of
facilitating the calculation of the standard deviation of T1% value. The differences in the T1% values
estimated using either choice of independent variable were generally small.

 21

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

3.5 Electrical Conductivity

 The electrical conductivity (EC) of HLW glasses was determined by measuring the
impedance of the glass melt at temperatures around 950, 1050, 1150 and 1250ºC as a function of
alternate current frequency using a calibrated platinum-rhodium electrode probe attached to a
Hewlett-Packard model 4194A or an Agilent 4294A impedance analyzer. The collected
impedance data were analyzed to obtain the direct current EC. The probe (analyzer along with
the crucible to assure that the geometry is replicated) was calibrated and checked periodically
using NIST traceable standard reference materials.

 The current WTP requirement is that glass melt EC be in the range of 0.1 to
0.7 Siemens/cm at 1100 to 1200 ºC [35].

3.6 Viscosity

 The melt viscosity (η) of each HLW glass was measured using a Brookfield viscometer
with a platinum-rhodium spindle and crucible. The relative torque of a rotating spindle immersed
in molten glass was measured as a function of rotational velocity (revolutions per minute (RPM))
at temperatures around 950, 1050, 1150 and 1250ºC. The viscosity of the molten glass was then
calculated from the collected data of torque versus RPM. The equipment was calibrated using
viscosity standard oils and checked periodically using a NIST traceable standard reference glass.

 Per current WTP requirements, glass melts should satisfy the viscosity limits of 10 to 150
poise at 1100 ºC [35].

3.7 Toxicity Characteristic Leaching Procedure (TCLP)

 The TCLP was performed on glass samples according to Environmental Protection
Agency (EPA) SW-846 Method 1311. This test determines the leach resistance of crushed glass
(<3/8”) in a sodium acetate buffer solution after 18 hours at 22°C with constant end-over-end
agitation. A mass of about 100 g of glass is leached in 2 L of TCLP extraction solution,
according to the method for non-volatiles. The surface area to volume ratio for this test is about
20 m-1, which is about two orders of magnitude lower than that in the PCT. The leachate
concentrations were measured by DCP-AES.

3.8 Nepheline Formation on Canister Centerline Cooling (CCC)

 Glass samples that underwent CCC heat treatment were distinguished from the original
glass samples by adding the extension “CCC” to the sample identifications. The CCC
temperature profile was provided by the WTP project [36]. Table 3.1 reproduces the HLW CCC
temperature profile. As is the case with isothermal heat treatment, the glass samples in platinum
or platinum alloy crucibles were maintained at a “pre-melt” temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour

 22

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

before initiation of the CCC treatment. At the end of the heat treatment, the samples were
recovered and processed for characterization by either SEM/EDS or XRD (see Section 3.4).

 23

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 SECTION 4.0
 HLW GLASS CHARACTERIZATION DATA

 As summarized in Section 2, the present enhancement phase incorporates an additional
137 HLW glasses into the database, including 27 actively designed glasses and 65 statistically
designed glasses (consisting of 5 CCC near replicates) from previous studies at VSL since 2015;
the remaining 45 statistically designed HLW16 glasses are from the present work. These glasses
are collectively referred to as ORP-2016 glasses. Table 4.1 lists these ORP-2016 glasses and the
property data collected for them. Table 4.1 shows that only a subset of the ORP-2016 glasses
have been characterized with respect to the selected properties. This is partly because the
objectives of the earlier statistical studies were to explore development of models to predict
phosphate liquidus temperature [27] and to support the development of nepheline prediction
model for HLW glasses [28]. Phosphate crystallization and nepheline formation are not subjects
of the WTP baseline HLW models. Overall, only 67 glasses from the earlier studies have
measured data for at least one of the following properties: PCT, electrical conductivity, viscosity,
spinel T1%, TCLP-Cd release, and nepheline formation. That number is further reduced to 56 if
only properties predicted by WTP baseline models are included (i.e., excluding nepheline
formation). In contrast, efforts were made to collect data on all properties for each of the 45
HLW16 matrix glasses. Data are available for most of the listed properties for all HLW16
glasses with the primary exception being spinel T1% because the primary crystalline phase is not
spinel in some cases or because crystallinity data available were insufficient for T1% estimation.
In addition, viscosity data were unavailable for 5 HLW16 glasses because they exhibited non-
Newtonian behavior during measurements. This section presents and discusses the PCT, T1%,
electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP-Cd release, and nepheline formation results.

 The development of HLW baseline property-composition models for WTP and their
enhancements for ORP both involve multi-year efforts and multiple test matrices. Table 4.2
compares the number of glasses that were collected in each of the HLW glass datasets according
to the property of interest. Glasses from the latest combined ORP dataset, as shown in Table 4.2,
outnumber those from the WTP dataset. However, it should be noted that the ORP dataset also
continues to expand the HLW compositional space.

 All glasses from the HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA16 matrices were characterized
compositionally by XRF before collection of property data. Selected glasses were also
characterized by DCP-AES, primarily to confirm the B2O3 and Li2O contents in the glasses.
Tables 4.3 and 4.4 summarize, respectively, the XRF and DCP-AES data.

4.1 Product Consistency Test (PCT) Results

 Table 4.1 shows that, of the 137 ORP-2016 glasses, 80 have data on PCT-boron (PCT-B)
and PCT-sodium (PCT-Na) releases while only 77 have data on PCT-lithium (PCT-Li) releases,

 24

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

because 3 glasses do not contain Li2O. Five of these glasses underwent CCC heat treatment and
are considered near replicates. Table 4.5 summarizes the PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na releases
for the ORP-2016 glasses together with the pH values measured of the leachates. Table 4.5 also
gives the normalized PCT concentrations (g/L) and mass losses (g/m2), as calculated using target
mass fractions of boron, lithium, and sodium in Equations (3.1) and (3.2).

 The normalized PCT-B releases for the ORP-2016 glasses vary from 0.290 g/L to
19.155 g/L, normalized PCT-Li releases vary from 0.336 g/L to 10.832 g/L, and normalized
PCT-Na releases vary from 0.309 g/L to 15.658 g/L. These ranges can be compared to the WTP
contract limits set by the DWPF-EA glass of PCT-B 16.695 g/L; PCT-Li 9.565 g/L; and PCT-Na
13.346 g/L.

 Figure 4.1 shows the ranges and median values of the normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and
PCT-Na releases (g/L) measured for the ORP-2016 glasses. Comparison can be made with the
results obtained for the WTP glasses [2], the ORP-2012 glasses [22], ORP-2013 glasses [23],
and ORP-2014 glasses [24]. The PCT release ranges and median values are generally very
similar among the various glass sets. Most of the HLW glasses have PCT releases beneath the
respective contract limits. For the ORP-2016 glass set, only two glasses exceed each of the
PCT-B limit (HLW-CP-18 and HLW16-UHA29) and the PCT-Li limit (HLW-CP-13 and
HLW16-UHA29). The glass HLW-CP-18 also does not meet the contract limit for PCT-Na
release. It is of interest to note that many of the ultra-high alumina glasses have relatively high
PCT releases (e.g., HLW16-UHA28 and -UHA29, both with 29 wt% Al2O3).

 Figure 4.2 displays the distribution of the normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na
releases for the ORP-2016 glasses. The predominance of glasses with low PCT releases has been
repeatedly observed with the earlier data sets, showing that PCT is not a very constraining
requirement and that the vast majority of HLW glasses, including statistically designed ones,
consistently outperform the DWPF-EA reference glass. Figure 4.3 shows a scatterplot matrix of
the natural logarithms of the PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na normalized releases for the 80
ORP-2016 glasses. It is seen that PCT releases for the HLW16-MA matrix glasses are in general
lower than those for the ultra-high alumina HLW16-UHA glasses. Deviations from congruency
among the PCT-releases are more apparent at high leachate concentrations.

4.2 One-Percent Crystal Fraction Temperature (T1%) Results

 Heat treatment of HLW glasses was performed between 700°C and 1100°C, at regular
temperature intervals of 50°C. The measured crystal vol% data and major crystalline phase(s) for
the HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA glasses are given in Table 4.6. Heat treatment data for the
other ORP-2016 glasses have been reported previously [26-29]. Spinel was the principal
crystalline phase observed in both the HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA series, in spite of the
large differences in the alumina concentrations. These two glass series can be contrasted with the
Phase 2 test matrices (HLW14- and HLW14-UH), which displayed a much reduced propensity
for formation of secondary phases [24]. Of particular interest were the compositions of the spinel
phases that crystallized in many of the HLW16-UHA glasses. Figure 4.4 shows a SEM
micrograph example of HLW16-UHA13 heat treated at 800°C for 70 hours. An EDS spectrum of

 25

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

the spinel crystal identified its composition to include Al, Cr, Fe, Ni, Mn, and Zn. Both natural
and synthetic aluminate spinels are known and have the general composition of MAl2O4 (M =
divalent cations such as Fe, Mg, Mn, Zn, etc.). In contrast, spinel crystals resulting from heat
treatment of HLW16-MA glasses contained primarily Fe, Cr, Mn, and Ni but no Al. The present
results demonstrate Al2O3 to be an important component in spinel crystallization for ultra-high
alumina glasses and suggest that, in addition to nepheline formation, spinel crystallization is an
important waste-loadings constraint in these glasses. Nepheline, apatite, and small amounts of
zirconium oxide were also observed in select heat treated HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA
glasses.

 Linear regression of the heat-treatment data provided estimates of T1% values for the
ORP-2016 glasses, fitting the temperature and vol% crystallinity data to an equation of the form

 T = ao + a1 v

where v is the vol% crystallinity at temperature T (°C), and ao and a1 are the fitted intercept and
slope. The typical correlation observed between crystal vol% and heat treatment temperature was
relatively simple and could be adequately described by the linear equation above. In a few cases,
the appearance of a second (or more) crystalline phase resulted in an abrupt change of the linear
relationship. The T1% values in those cases were estimated based on the linear trend defined by
crystallization of the primary phase(s), instead of total vol% crystallinity. Table 4.7 presents the
fitted intercepts and slopes for the ORP-2016 glasses together with the estimated T1% values and
the number of regression data points.

 Table 4.7 gives the estimated T1% values for 50 ORP-2016 glasses, including 34
HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA glasses. Regression was not performed for glasses with
insufficient crystallization data and glasses that crystallized nepheline. Regression was
performed for HLW16-UHA26 and -UHA28 but they are excluded from Table 4.7 because their
T1% values were estimated by large extrapolations (T1% > 1430ºC vs. 1100ºC, the highest heat
treatment temperature). All 50 glasses in Table 4.7 crystallized spinel as the primary phase
(HLW16-MA03 also formed apatite as a secondary phase).

 The estimated spinel T1% values span a range of 667.0°C to 1221.1°C, with a median of
921.0°C. These can be compared with the WTP processing requirement of T1% ≤ 950°C. Figure
4.5 is a histogram that shows the distribution of the estimated spinel T1% values for the
ORP-2016 glasses.

4.3 Electrical Conductivity Results

 Of the 137 ORP-2016 glasses discussed in Section 2, 73 have electrical conductivity (EC)
results, as summarized in Table 4.1. The combined WTP and ORP database now consists of 525
HLW glasses with EC data (Table 4.2). The EC versus temperature data for the ORP-2016
glasses are given in Table 4.8.

 26

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 The EC versus temperature data in Table 4.8 vary from 0.074 S/cm (glass HLW16-MA01
at 983°C) to 0.988 S/cm (A104D4-06 at 1263°C). These values are compared with the EC data
from earlier datasets in Table 4.9. They can also be compared with the WTP requirement for
HLW glass melts: the EC should be within 0.1 S/cm to 0.7 S/cm between 1100°C and 1200°C.
For measurements taken in this temperature range, the EC results are all within the acceptable
range except for A104D4-06 (0.765 S/cm at 1165°C). This glass supported testing of the direct
feed option for vitrifying the fully washed A-104 tank solids and contained an unusually high
concentration of Li2O + Na2O, which was added to suppress spinel crystallization.

 Figure 4.6 displays the distribution of temperature values at which data were collected for
the ORP-2016 glasses with EC data. It shows that the measurement temperatures are higher than
the four nominal temperatures (950°C, 1000°C, 1150°C, and 1250°C). Deviations from the
nominal temperatures are moderate (6°C to 36°C) and do not affect the suitability of the data for
model development [2]. Figure 4.7 graphically presents the EC data as a function of
measurement temperature.

4.4 Viscosity Results

 Of the 137 ORP-2016 glasses discussed in Section 2, 73 were characterized for melt
viscosity. During viscosity measurement for 5 of these glasses (HLW16-UHA06, -UHA18,
-UHA24, HLW16-MA07 and -MA16), unstable and non-Newtonian behavior was observed,
especially at lower measurement temperatures when crystallization was apparent. Viscosity data
could be collected only at fewer than four temperatures for these glasses and these data were not
added to the database since glasses with fewer than four viscosity data points were excluded
from previous model development [2]. As a result, only 68 ORP-2016 glasses have viscosity data,
as summarized in Table 4.10. The combined WTP and ORP database now consists of 498 HLW
glasses with viscosity data (Table 4.2).

 Table 4.10 gives the 272 viscosity versus temperature data points for the 68 OPR-2016
glasses. The measured data vary between 6.3 P (HLW-CP-22 at 1263°C) and 4925.1 P
(HLW16-UHA20 at 957°C). These values are compared with the viscosity data collected for
earlier datasets in Table 4.11. They can also be compared with the recommended WTP limit of
150 P at 1100°C [35] for HLW glass melts. Twenty glasses have viscosities that exceed the
recommended limit, with interpolated values of 151 to 468 P at 1100°C, including 16 HLW16-
UHA glasses and 1 HLW16-MA glass. They are A104D1-03, HLW15-18, HLW15-UH11,
HLW16-UHA01, -UHA02, -UHA03, -UHA04, -UHA07, -UHA08, -UHA09, -UHA12, -UHA13,
-UHA14, -UHA15, -UHA16, -UHA17, -UHA20, -UHA22, -UHA23, -UHA28, and HLW16-
MA01. There are no ORP-2016 glasses with viscosities beneath the recommended lower
processing limit of 10 P at 1100°C.

 Figure 4.8 displays the distribution of temperature values at which data were collected for
the ORP-2016 glasses with viscosity data. It shows that the measurement temperatures are
generally very close to the four nominal temperatures (950°C, 1000°C, 1150°C, and 1250°C),
with deviations ranging from -7°C to 14°C. Any variation in measurement temperature do not

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R4220-1, Rev. 0

affect the suitability of the data for model development [2]. Figure 4.9 graphically presents the
viscosity data as a function of measurement temperature.

4.5 TCLP-Cd release Results

 Of the 137 ORP-2016 glasses discussed in Section 2, only the statistically designed
matrix glasses (i.e., HLW-CP-, HLW15-, HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA series) contain CdO as
a glass component. Further, only the 45 HLW16-UHA and HLW16-MA glasses underwent
TCLP testing for Cd-release. The CdO concentration in the inner layer HLW16-UHA glasses
and the HLW16-MA glasses is 1.0 wt%. For the outer layer HLW16-UHA16 glasses, it is
0.25 wt%. Table 4.12 gives the measured TCLP-Cd release data (cCd, mg/L) for these glasses; it
also gives the normalized Cd-release data (rCd, g/L), which were calculated according to the
equation:

 cCd (mg / L)
             rCd ( g / L)                                                                   ,
                              [1000(mg / g )][mCdO (g CdO / g glass )][0.8754(g Cd / g CdO)]

where mCdO is the mass fraction of CdO in glass. Both cCd and rCd were considered in developing
the different TCLP-Cd release models [1].

 The 45 glasses in Table 4.12 have Cd-releases that range from 0.11 to 5.42 mg/L for the
unnormalized data (cCd) and 0.022 to 0.619 g/L for the normalized data (rCd). These are in
general larger when compared to those from earlier modeling studies. For example, the
corresponding ranges of TCLP-Cd releases for the 100 WTP glasses are 0.01 to 1.25 mg/L and
0.01 to 0.12 g/L [1]. In addition, there are 22 ORP-2016 glasses with Cd-releases above the
delisting limit of 0.48 mg/L. This can be compared with only 28 glasses from the previous
combined WTP/ORP dataset of 256 glasses. The baseline WTP model for Cd-release prediction
shows good performance only up to 0.25 mg/L and tends to under-predict for larger releases [1].
The ORP-2016 Cd-release data therefore will be valuable in extending and improving the WTP
baseline model.

4.6 Nepheline Formation Results

 Nepheline formation in high alumina glasses can be rather extensive since the nepheline
constituents are generally present in large amounts in these glasses. Nepheline formation during
CCC can lead to large increases in PCT releases in some high alumina glasses, rendering them
unacceptable for HLW disposal. The development work for the baseline WTP models did not
include a model to control the formation of nepheline upon CCC [2]. Instead, the approach
employed at the DWPF to limit nepheline formation was employed for the WTP Preliminary
IHLW Formulation Algorithm [37]. The nepheline discriminator (NSi) developed by Li et al. [38,
39] is used as a process control constraint at DWPF. It is based on the phase boundary of
nepheline primary field in the Na2O-Al2O3-SiO2 ternary system. Glasses with NSi =
SiO2/(Na2O+Al2O3+SiO2) ≥ 0.62 are predicted not to form nepheline during CCC whereas
glasses with NSi < 0.62 are predicted to form nepheline.

 28

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 Findings from the Phase 1 and Phase 2 modeling work and many other studies have
repeatedly shown that, while the nepheline discriminator is applicable to many HLW glasses, it
is unduly conservative in that it would reject high alumina glasses that do not form nepheline on
CCC heat treatment [12, 13, 16, 23, 24, 28], thereby limiting the achievable waste loadings.
Development of new nepheline models was explored at VSL using different statistical
approaches that are suited for categorical data (e.g., yes/no) with the WTP/ORP combined
dataset and other glass data [28]. The new models show considerable improvements in
misclassification rates over the nepheline discriminator and low false negative rates. Efforts are
continuing to further develop and enhance the nepheline prediction models [40], which will
require expanding the dataset available for model development. As summarized in Table 4.1, the
present work collected nepheline formation data for 80 ORP-2016 glasses. Table 4.13 presents
the crystallinity data upon CCC heat treatment, which include estimated total vol% crystallinity,
identity of the major crystalline phase(s) and, when applicable and available, vol% of nepheline.
Of the 80 ORP-2016 glasses, 20 formed nepheline after CCC heat treatment, including 8
HLW16-UHA glasses. Nepheline formation was not observed with any of the HLW16-MA
glasses. The combined WTP/ORP database now contains 336 glasses with data on nepheline
formation (Table 4.2).

 Figure 4.10 relates the NSi values of ORP-2016 glasses to the vol% nepheline formed
during CCC heat treatment. Only 9 of the 80 glasses have NSi values ≥ 0.62, all of which were
correctly predicted by the nepheline discriminator not to form nepheline. Of the remaining 71
glasses with NSi values < 0.62, however, only 20 formed nepheline, demonstrating once again
that the model is strongly biased towards false positives and is therefore very conservative.

4.7 Replicate Data

 As described in Section 2.2, the HLW16-MA matrix was designed to include a replicate
pair of glasses (HLW16-MA10 and -MA11). The design, however, did not include replicates for
matrix glasses from the previous WTP and ORP modeling studies. The WTP modeling datasets
include up to 26 sets of replicate and near-replicate glasses. Near-replicate glasses typically have
very small differences for some composition variables. The small differences arise from
rounding or renormalization when the glasses involved are from different studies. This
subsection summarizes the test data for the true replicate glasses in the HLW16-MA matrix and
compares them with data for the replicate glasses from earlier tests.

 During Phase 1 of this work, 2 replicate glasses were tested, including one from a high
alumina matrix developed for testing at Pacific Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL). Data for
26 sets of WTP/ORP replicates were presented, together with calculated standard deviations
(SDs), percent relative standard deviations (%RSDs), and pooled %RSDs [23]. A pooled %RSD
combines the separate %RSD from each replicate set in order to provide a more representative
and accurate estimate of the %RSD. Table 4.14 gives the SDs and %RSDs for the replicate
glasses in the HLW16-MA matrix for the normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na releases.
These data were used with earlier data to revise the pooled SDs and %RSDs for the combined 27
sets of WTP/ORP replicates. Large changes in the revised SD and %RSD values are not

 29

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

expected since only one replicate is included in the present study. Table 4.14 shows that the
uncertainty estimates for the PCT releases based on the new replicate are considerably lower
than those obtained from more numerous replicates from previous WTP and ORP testing. This is
not unexpected since the pooled SDs and %RSDs were collected over a period of over 10 years
and contain many additional long-term sources of variation. Similar observations can be made
for the SDs and %RSDs found for spinel T1% (Table 4.15), EC (Table 4.16) and viscosity (Table
4.17).

 Testing in replicate for TCLP-Cd release had not been performed at VSL previously.
Replicate glasses from earlier matrices were tested at two accredited environmental laboratories
to support delisting compliance (Severn Trent Laboratories (STL) at Knoxville, TN and STL at
St. Louis, MO) [1]. The pooled %RSD and SD for STL-St. Louis are about three times larger
than the pooled values for STL-Knoxville. Table 4.18 gives the SD and %RSD determined for
the TCLP-Cd releases from HLW16-MA10 and -MA11 measured at VSL. These values are
more comparable to those for STL-Knoxville and are considerably smaller than those for STL-St.
Louis.

 30

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 SECTION 5.0
 HLW PROPERTY-COMPOSITION MODELS

 Test objectives of the present work include assessment of the HLW property-composition
models with the expanded datasets and development of enhanced models. This section
documents the revision of the Phase 2 HLW property-composition models for predicting various
HLW glass properties using the updated and combined WTP, ORP-2012, ORP-2013, ORP-2014,
and ORP-2016 datasets. Preliminary models were developed during Phase 1 and Phase 2 of this
work for PCT-B, PCT-Li, PCT-Na, T1%, melt viscosity, melt electrical conductivity, and
Cd-TCLP [23, 24]; models for nepheline formation were not addressed in that work because that
is the subject of a separate task [28]. Development of models with new model forms has also
been explored for selected properties and their performances are compared with the respective
revised models in this section.

 As expansion of the database continues in order to support processing of high waste
loading HLW glass formulations at WTP, both in terms of the number of glasses and the
compositional regions, property-composition models need to be updated to incorporate the new
data. In addition to revising the WTP baseline models, Phase 1 and Phase 2 work explored the
development of local models, which made use of only a subset of the combined WTP/ORP
database. The WTP baseline models are global models in that each model uses a single model
form over the entire glass composition region of interest. As additional data over a wider
composition region become available, global models may not be adequate to approximate the
relationship between a glass property and glass composition while local models may be more
accurate with lower uncertainties over smaller compositional regions [2]. This is especially
applicable to the greatly expanded glass compositional regions afforded by the ORP datasets,
where non-linear blending effects of HLW glass components on various properties (including
PCT releases, spinel T1%, and Cd-TCLP) may be very complicated. The approach adopted in
Phase 1 and Phase 2 to develop local models involved dividing the WTP/ORP dataset into two
subsets: high-alumina glasses (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and low-alumina glasses (≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3).
As noted above, this choice of division was made over other choices because: (i) while the WTP
glasses were developed primarily for iron-limited wastes, many of the ORP glasses were
formulated specifically for the aluminum- and the aluminum/sodium-limited wastes; (ii) the
maximum component limit for Al2O3 in the WTP baseline model validity region is 13.1 wt%;
and (iii) a design feature for the Phase 1 test glass (high-alumina augmentation) matrix limits the
Al2O3 content to a minimum of 13.1 wt%. The two test matrices in this work were designed to
augment the moderate alumina region (HLW16-MA, 9 to 12 wt% Al2O3) and the ultra-high
alumina region (HLW16-UHA, 26 to 29 wt% Al2O3). The present phase of model development
continues to consider both global and local models. Moreover, the division of the WTP/ORP
dataset according to a set with ≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3 and another with > 13.1 wt% Al2O3 was
retained in updating the local models.

 Another option that may be considered to improve the performance of the WTP baseline
models is to develop models of different forms. Complicated non-linear blending effects of glass

 31

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

components are known to exist over the WTP HLW glass composition region. For example, the
effect of alumina on PCT responses has been reported to be non-linear and, at low concentrations,
alumina improves glass durability with respect to PCT, but levels off at intermediate
concentrations before decreasing the durability at the highest concentrations [41-45]. Models
with different forms (e.g., cubic polynomial-type) may be used to better represent the property-
composition relationships than the WTP baseline partial quadratic mixture (PQM) models.
Development of models based on new model forms for PCT releases and spinel T1% has been
explored and the results are presented below.

 The data collected on ORP-2016 glasses for PCT, spinel T1%, electrical conductivity,
viscosity, Cd-TCLP and nepheline formation are presented in Section 4. Table 5.1 provides a
summary of the number of HLW glasses available to support model revisions for each of the
properties of interest, including division of the glasses based on alumina concentrations into
Group 1 (≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and Group 2 (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3). In spite of the augmentation
designed to bridge the compositional gaps in the high alumina region, it can be seen in Table 5.1
that considerably more glasses are available in Group 1 to support modeling than in Group 2. It
is therefore expected that addition of more Group 2 data will be required in the future.

 The results from model revisions using the updated dataset are presented and discussed in
the following subsections. Summaries of the modeling parameters and performance of the
various models that were studied for each property are compiled in tables in each subsection. The
statistical terms that are used in modeling are described in Appendix C of the WTP baseline
model report [2]. They are reproduced below for convenience.

 The following notations are used in the subsequent descriptions and definitions:

 n = the number of data points used to fit the model,

 p = the number of parameters (coefficients) in the model form estimated via regression on
 the data,

 yi = the measured property value (or, frequently, the natural logarithm of the measured
 property value) for the ith data point,

 ŷ i = the predicted property value (or, frequently, the natural logarithm of the predicted
 property value) for the ith data point using the model fitted to all n data points,

 y = the average (mean) of the n measured property values (or, frequently, the average of
 the natural logarithm of the measured property values).

 The residual sum of squares (RSS), which would equal zero if the model was perfect (i.e.,
for all n data points, the predicted property value coincides perfectly with the measured value),
can be calculated using Equation 5.1. The RSS can be compared to the total variability in the
regression model, or the total sum of squares (TSS) expressed as the squared difference between
each measured property value yi and the average y :

 Generally, R2 statistics take values between 0 and 1. Negative values of R2 estimated on
the validation set or R2Adjusted reflect a poor fitting model, a model that contains more terms than
needed to fit the data, or a model fitted to data with one or more very influential data points.
More than a minor difference between R2 and R2Adjusted indicates that the model may contain
more terms than needed to achieve the same goodness of fit.

 If the fitted model is adequate, the root mean square error (RMSE) provides an estimate
of the standard deviation associated with melting glasses and measuring the associated property
values being modeled. RMSE is therefore expected to be comparable to the measured glass
preparation and property measurement standard deviation assessed from replicates testing. The
statistic RMSE is included as a standard output in the JMP® software (SAS Institute, Version
10.0) used in modeling, and is given by:

 Optimal prediction yields R2Predict closest to 1 but a poor predictive model can yield

 The statistical lack-of-fit (LOF) test checks whether the differences between measured
and predicted property values from a fitted model are larger than expected based on the
experimental and measurement uncertainty in the data. If the predicted versus measured
differences are larger than data uncertainty at a high enough statistical confidence (e.g., greater
than 90%), the model is said to have a statistically significant LOF. Replicate data points
containing all applicable sources of experimental and measurement uncertainty are required to
perform statistical LOF tests.

This process is conducted using a LOF F-test given by:

 The scatter among the replicates of the measured property (multiple yi for a single xi) is
calculated to assess the scatter of points around the best fit curve. The scatter around the
replicates is pooled into the sum of squares of pure error (SSPE). The sum of squares error (SSE)
is calculated for all of the model data points. The degree of freedom for SSE is np; and the
