 The p-value of the F-test (F probability distribution) is used to assess whether the fitted
model has a statistically significant LOF for the modeling dataset. As the LOF p-value becomes
smaller, the indication that the model is inadequate becomes greater. A threshold LOF p-value of
0.05 is traditionally used to assess whether the fitted model has a statistically significant LOF
(significant at the 95% confidence level) for the modeling dataset and that a different model
might be required.

5.1 PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na Model Updates

 5.1.1 PCT Dataset

 The combined WTP/ORP dataset contains 600 glasses with data on PCT boron (PCT-B)
releases (Table 5.1). For PCT lithium (PCT-Li) and PCT sodium releases, the numbers are 558
and 602, respectively. Screening of the ORP-2016 glasses for outliers was not performed and all
PCT data were used in revising the respective models. Table 5.1 shows that there are
approximately the same numbers of Group 1 and Group 2 glasses from the OPR dataset available
for developing local models. Since all WTP glasses belong to Group 1, there continues to be
more glasses with Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt% available for modeling than those with Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%.

 During development of the WTP baseline models, model validation was accomplished by
data-splitting and data-partitioning. In the data-splitting approach, the modeling dataset was split
into five subsets and about 80% of the data were used for modeling and the remaining 20% for
validation. Five sets of modeling and validation statistics were reported together with the average.
The data-splitting approach has the advantage of employing all the available data to develop the
model while getting close to validating the models of interest (i.e., models fitted to all glasses).
In the data-partitioning approach, the modeling dataset was partitioned into a modeling subset
and a validation subset. The modeling dataset, which comprised of statistically designed glasses
(and selected actively designed glasses), was used in model development but not validation.
Model validation was performed on the validation dataset, which comprised of actively designed

 35

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

glasses. Actively designed glasses are less suitable than statistically designed glasses for model
development since they are generally more clustered in composition space and can have strong
correlations between glass components.

 During Phase 1 and Phase 2 of this work, k-fold cross validation was used for model
validation. In k-fold validation, the statistical analysis software JMP® splits the dataset into k
subsets. Each of the subsets is used to validate the model obtained by fitting with the rest of the
data, giving a total of k models and validations. Similar to data-splitting, this approach allows
maximum use of the available data while maintaining a validation set. Model development was
performed in Phase 1 and Phase 2 with k = 5.

 The present work made use of both data-partitioning and k-fold validation. For
development of global models, the ORP-2016 glasses were divided into a modeling subset and a
validation subset. The modeling subset consists of all the modeling glasses from the data-
partitioning validation approach during development of the WTP baseline models, which
included the statistically designed matrix glasses (HLW02, HLW03, and HLW07) with addition
of selected other glasses to provide better coverage of certain compositional regions [2].
Statistically designed glasses from Phase 1, Phase 2, and the present phase (HLW13, HLW13A,
HLW14, HLW14-UH, HLW16-UHA, and HLW-MA) were also collected into the present
modeling subset, as were some actively designed glasses that were randomly chosen such that
about 20% of all glasses available were used in validation. As a result, there are 464 glasses in
the modeling subset and 136 glasses in the validation subset for PCT-B. The corresponding
numbers are 434 and 124 for PCT-Li; and 465 and 137 for PCT-Na. Appendix A provides a
summary of the validation glasses.

 For development of local models, the data-partitioning approach was not followed
because the availability of data became more limited after splitting the glasses into two groups
according to alumina concentration. It was decided that the full sets of data would be used to
develop the local models and only k-fold cross validation was used for the local models that were
developed (k =5).

 The PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na data collected for the OPR-2016 glasses are
summarized in Section 4. As noted above, it is desirable for modeling to have more glasses with
higher PCT releases than indicated in Figure 4.2. It is also noted that Figure 4.3 shows that ultra-
high alumina glasses (HLW16-UHA) have higher PCT releases than those with moderate
alumina content (HLW16-MA), illustrating the complicated effects of Al2O3 on PCT leaching
[41-45].

 5.1.2 Development of PCT Models

 Four Phase 3 models were developed for the PCT release of each of the three elements
(i.e., B, Li, and Na) in this work. They include two global models and two local models.
Development of the global modes employed the modeling data subset and their validation was
achieved using both the validation subset and k-fold cross validation. In contrast, development of
the Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Local 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) models made use of all

 36

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R4220-1, Rev. 0

available data in modeling; data-partition validation was not performed in that case.
Development of the Phase 3 PCT models closely followed the earlier model development
approaches and made use of the baseline models as starting points [2, 24]. Specifically, based on
component distributions of the HLW glasses, the WTP development effort determined that 24
mixture components have sufficient ranges and distribution to support model terms. These were
Al2O3, B2O3, CaO, CdO, Cr2O3, Fe2O3, K2O, La2O3, Li2O, MgO, MnO, Na2O, NiO, PbO, Sb2O3,
SeO2, SiO2, SrO, ThO2, TiO2, Tl2O, UO3, ZnO, and ZrO2. After addition of the ORP glasses into
the combined dataset, Bi2O3 was also included. These oxide components and “Others” (the sum
of all remaining components in glass) were used in initial PCT modeling. The ORP-2016 dataset
introduces another oxide, Nb2O5, for addition into the “Others”. The WTP baseline PCT models
and their subsequent revisions were all partial quadratic PQM models. The WTP baseline models
also include an extra term for bias correction. The Phase 3 modeling effort also considered PQM
model forms for the global and local models using natural logarithmic transformations of PCT
normalized releases with the following model form but no bias correction (the advantages of
using natural transformation in modeling have been discussed previously [2]):

 q q q 1 q 
 ln( C B , C Li or C Na )   bi xi  Selected   bii xi2    bij xi x j  , (5.9)
                                               i 1               i 1           i j         

where ln(CB), ln(CLi), and ln(CNa) are the natural logarithms of normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and
PCT-Na, respectively, the xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) is the normalized mass fraction of the q glass oxide
 q
such that  xi  1 , and the bi, bii and bij are coefficients to be estimated. Modeling began by
 i 1

fitting the PCT data to a first-order model with no quadratic terms. The fitting results determined
the selection of linear modeling terms in the following manner. The 99%-confidence intervals of
the model parameter estimates for the linear terms were compared against the mean response. If
the mean response fell within the confidence levels, it was an indication that the property was not
significantly affected by the model term [46, 47], which was then eliminated from the model.
This approach was similar to that adopted in the development of LAW property-composition
models at VSL [48]. Components that were determined to be of insufficient significance were
included in “Others”. The nonlinear terms in the components of the PQM model were then
evaluated using JMP® Response Surface methodologies 1 on the full dataset. The Response
Surface (RS) method was employed in order to identify both the significant cross-product and
square terms. Stepwise regression allowed selection of the significant nonlinear terms based on
the p-values calculated in the Student t-tests2. The regression continued until all nonlinear terms
in the model had p-values lower than 0.05.

1
  JMP offers two Response Surface Methodologies providing methods of augmenting a linear mixture model with
quadratic terms or other nonlinear blending terms. The Mixture Response Surface (MRS) model suppresses the
intercept, includes all the linear main-effect terms, excludes all the square terms (such as), but includes all cross
terms (such as X1×X2). The Response Surface (RS) is different from MRS only by including all square terms
(X1×X1).
2
  The t-test was used in a stepwise regression process to identify important cross-terms in a model while linear terms
were selected on the basis of their confidence intervals. The mixed direction (alternating forward and backward
steps) approach was selected. The t-test computes a t-statistic equal to a model coefficient divided by its standard
deviation. The t-statistic is then compared to the t probability distribution to determine the probability of getting a
t-statistic at least as large as a pre-determined limit. The resulting probability is referred to as a p-value and

 37

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 In addition to the RS methodologies discussed above, JMP® also provides a method to
further augment linear mixture (LM) model with cubic terms to capture the non-linear blending
effects. Similar to the Mixture Response Surface (MRS) model, the Scheffé Cubic model
suppresses the intercept and excludes all the square terms such as X1×X1, but includes cubic
terms of the form X1×X2×X3 and X1×X2×(X1–X2). The Scheffé Cubic model form is given by

 q q q q 
  ln( C B , C Li or C Na )   bi xi  Selected   bij xi x j   bij xi x j ( xi  x j )   bijk xi x j x k  (5.10)
                             i 1               i  j           i j                       i  j k           

 The Scheffé Cubic model was considered in the present work for developing global
models only. Evaluation and selection of the linear terms in this case was identical to the
approach employed in developing the PQM models, while the Scheffé Cubic method was used to
identify the significant cubic terms.

 5.1.3 Phase 3 PCT Models

PCT-B Models

 The Phase 3 global model for ln(PCT-B) release developed with 464 glasses is a 29-term
PQM model, with 11 linear and 18 non-linear terms. Table 5.2 presents the coefficients of the
model, coefficient standard deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and performance
statistics for the modeling and k-fold validation. Table 5.2 also includes the Phase 2 global model
for comparison.

 In Table 5.2, the model evaluation statistics R2 = 0.830, R2A = 0.819, R2P = 0.799, and
RMSE = 0.403 for the Phase 3 model are all slight improvements over the corresponding
statistics for the Phase 2 model [24]. They are also better than those for the WTP baseline PCT-B
model [2]. For the validation subset of 136 glasses, the validation statistic R2V = 0.621 is
comparable to that obtained from the WTP data-partitioning validation approach (R2V = 0.618).
It is, however, poorer when compared to the k-fold validation R2 of 0.792; this may be attributed
to the non-representative partitioning of the data. Figure 5.1 shows the predicted versus
measured plot for 464 modeling glasses for the Phase 3 global PCT-B model and Figure 5.2
shows similar plot for the 136 validation glasses. Figure 5.1 indicates that both Group 1 (Al2O3 ≤
13.1 wt%) and Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) glasses have very similar PCT-B release ranges.
Figure 5.2 shows that the model tends to (i) over-predict for PCT-B releases up to about
-0.5 ln (g/l) and (ii) under-predict PCT-B releases above approximately 1.0 ln(g/l). A similar
observation was made during development of previous models [2, 24]. In addition, there is a
general shortage of validation data for Group 2 glasses and in the high PCT-B release region.

represents the probability of incorrectly deciding a coefficient is significantly different than zero. JMP outputs
estimated model coefficients, coefficients standard errors, t-statistics, and p-values. The smaller the p-value the
stronger is the evidence that the coefficient is significantly different from zero, and that the corresponding model
term is needed. To avoid running models with too many potentially unnecessary terms, JMP evaluates p-values for
the coefficients in a “full” model, and the model term whose coefficient is statistically insignificant is not included.

 38

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 Table 5.3 summarizes the modeling results for the global Scheffé Cubic model for PCT-B
release developed with the same subset of 464 glasses. It has 4 more modeling terms than the
Phase 3 global PQM model. The associated modeling statistics are all similar to those for the
PQM model. The validation statistics R2V = 0.558 and R2k-fold = 0.776, however, compare less
favorably with those from the PQM model. Hence, addition of cubic model terms may not offer
any improvement or advantages over the PQM model for PCT-B release. Figure 5.3 shows the
predicted versus measured plot for 464 modeling glasses for the Phase 3 global Scheffé Cubic
PCT-B model and Figure 5.4 shows similar plot for the 136 validation glasses.

 Table 5.4 summarizes and compares the Phase 2 and Phase 3 Local 1 PCT-B models
developed for the Group 1 glasses. The Phase 3 Local 1 model is a 29-term PQM model. As seen
in Table 5.4, the Phase 3 modeling and k-fold validation R2 statistics are somewhat better. They
are also very comparable to those for the WTP baseline model, which was developed for 264
Group 1 glasses. The predicted versus measured plot for the Phase 3 Local 1 mode is shown in
Figure 5.5.

 The number of Group 2 HLW glasses with PCT-B data available for modeling increased
from 118 in Phase 2 to 166 in Phase 3. The Local 2 models developed from both Phases are
given in Table 5.5. The Phase 3 Local 2 model is a 17-term PQM model with eight linear terms.
Both the goodness-of-fit and k-fold validation statistics are very similar for the two Local 2
models. When compared to the WTP baseline PCT-B model (developed for Group 1 glasses),
both Local 2 models (developed for Group 2 glasses) have significantly better modeling and
validation statistics. Figure 5.6 displays the predicted versus measured plot for 166 modeling
glasses for the Phase 3 Local 2 model.

 The Phase 3 Local 2 PCT-B model performed better than the Phase 3 Global PCT-B
model but that is not the case for the Local 1 model, which is marginally worse. However, the
results suggest that the use of local models may have some advantages for this response for the
present dataset.

PCT-Li Models

 The Phase 3 global model for ln(PCT-Li) release developed with a subset of 434 glasses
is a 25-term PQM model, with 10 linear and 15 non-linear terms. Table 5.6 presents the
coefficients of the model, coefficient standard deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and
performance statistics for the modeling and k-fold validation. Table 5.6 also includes the Phase 2
global model for comparison.

 In Table 5.6, the model evaluation statistics R2 = 0.782, R2A = 0.769, R2P = 0.740, and
RMSE = 0.358 for the Phase 3 global model are all slight improvements over the corresponding
statistics for the Phase 2 model [24]. They are similar to those for the WTP baseline PCT-Li
model [2]. The validation performance for the validation subset of 124 glasses is relatively poor.
Although the R2V = 0.507 value is slightly better than that obtained for the WTP baseline model
(0.467), it shows that the model does not predict as well as suggested by the fitting statistics.
This may be ascribed to the non-representative partitioning of the data. Figure 5.7 displays the

 39

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

predicted versus measured plot for 434 modeling glasses for the Phase 3 global PCT-Li model
and Figure 5.8 shows similar plot for the 124 validation glasses. Figure 5.7 indicates that both
Group 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) glasses have very similar PCT-Li
release ranges. Figure 5.8 shows that the model tends to (i) over-predict for PCT-Li releases up
to about -0.5 ln (g/l) and (ii) under-predict PCT-Li releases above approximately 1.0 ln(g/l). In
addition, there is a general shortage of validation data for Group 2 glasses in the high PCT-Li

 The Scheffé Cubic global PCT-Li model is given in Table 5.7. The model contains 23

 Table 5.8 summarizes and compares the Phase 2 and Phase 3 Local 1 PCT-Li models
developed for the Group 1 glasses. Both models are 28-term PQM models but the Phase 3 model
has one less linear term (SrO was removed). Table 5.8 shows that the Phase 3 modeling statistics
are slightly worse but the k-fold validation R2 statistics are similar. The modeling and validation
statistics are very comparable to those for the WTP baseline model, which was developed with
250 Group 1 glasses. The predicted versus measured plot for the Phase 3 Local 1 PCT-Li model

 The number of Group 2 HLW glasses with PCT-B data available for modeling increased
from 112 in Phase 2 to 159 in Phase 3. The Local 2 models developed from both Phases are
given in Table 5.9. The Phase 3 Local 2 model is a 19-term PQM model with eight linear terms.
Both the goodness-of-fit and k-fold validation statistics for the Phase 3 model show
improvements over the Phase 2 model. When compared to the WTP baseline PCT-Li model
(developed for Group 1 glasses), both Local 2 models (developed for Group 2 glasses) have
significantly better modeling and validation statistics. Figure 5.12 displays the predicted versus
measured plot for 166 modeling glasses for the Phase 3 Local 2 model.

 The Phase 3 Local 2 PCT-Li model performed significantly better than the Phase 3
Global PCT-Li model but that is not the case for the Local 1 model, which is somewhat worse.
However, the results suggest that the use of local models may have some advantages for this
response for the present dataset.

5.2 Spinel T1% Models

 5.2.1 Spinel T1% Dataset

 The total number of HLW glasses available for spinel T1% modeling during Phase 2 was
392 [23]. The newly collected data described in Section 2 expanded this number to 442. As
shown in Section 4, many of the ultra-high alumina glasses (HLW16-UHA series) crystallized
aluminate-based spinel upon heat treatment, in contrast with the spinel crystals observed in
glasses with lower alumina concentrations, which are primarily iron-based. This is expected to
have important impacts on spinel T1% modeling. Table 5.1 provides a summary of the number of
HLW glasses available for spinel T1% modeling, including division of the glasses according to
their aluminum concentrations into Group 1 (≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and Group 2 (> 13.1 wt%
Al2O3). Table 5.1 shows that the ORP dataset contributed roughly the same number of glasses to
each of Group 1 and Group 2. However, there are still significantly more Group 1 glasses overall
available for modeling.

 As was the case with PCT modeling, development of Phase 3 global spinel T1% models
employed both data-partitioning and k-fold validation. The ORP-2016 glasses were partitioned
into a modeling subset and a validation subset. The modeling subset consists of all the modeling
glasses from the data-partitioning validation approach during development of the WTP baseline
models, which included the statistically designed matrix glasses (HLW02, HLW03, and HLW07)
with addition of selected other glasses to provide better coverage of certain compositional
regions [2]. Statistically designed glasses from Phase 1, Phase 2, and the present phase (HLW13,
HLW13A, HLW14, HLW14-UH, HLW16-UHA, and HLW-MA) were also collected into the
present modeling subset, as were some actively designed glasses that were randomly chosen. As
a result, there are 359 glasses in the modeling subset and 83 glasses in the validation subset.
Appendix A provides a summary of the validation glasses.

 5.2.2 Spinel T1% Model Development

 Four Phase 3 models were developed for spinel T1% in this work. They include two global
models and two local models. Development of the global models employed the modeling data
subset and their validation was achieved using both the validation subset and k-fold cross
validation. In contrast, development of the Local 1 (≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and Local 2 (> 13.1 wt%
Al2O3) models made use of all available data in modeling; data-partition validation was not
performed. Development of the Phase 3 spinel T1% models closely followed the earlier model
development approaches and made use of the baseline models as starting points [2, 24].
Specifically, the development considered the PQM model form for both the global and local
models. The PQM model form is given by

ai, aii, aij, and bi are coefficients to be estimated, and T1% is in °C.

 The modeling approach was similar to that used for PCT model development, where
comparison of the mean response value (962.4ºC for 359 glasses for global modeling, 947.0ºC
for Local 1 modeling, and 991.3ºC for Local 2 modeling) versus the 99%-confidence intervals of
the model parameter estimates was used to select the model linear terms. The non-linear terms in
the components of the PQM model were then evaluated using JMP®. The Response Surface (RS)
method was used to identify the significant cross-product and square terms. Stepwise regression
allowed selection of the significant non-linear terms based on the calculated p-values. The
regression continued until all non-linear terms in the model had p-values lower than 0.005 to
minimize the number of model terms.

 In addition to the RS methodologies, JMP® also provides a method to further augment
the LM model with cubic terms to capture the non-linear blending effects. Similar to the Mixture
Response Surface (MRS) model, the Scheffé Cubic model suppresses the intercept and excludes
all the square terms such as X1×X1, but includes cubic terms of the form X1×X2×X3 and
X1×X2×(X1–X2). The Scheffé Cubic model form is given by

 The Scheffé Cubic model was considered in the present work for developing global
models only. Evaluation and selection of the linear terms in this case was identical to the
approach employed in developing the PQM models, while the Scheffé Cubic method was used to
identify the significant cubic terms.

 5.2.3 Phase 3 Spinel T1% Models

 Table 5.14 presents the coefficients of the Phase 3 global (22-term PQM) model for
spinel T1% developed for 359 HLW glasses. The table also contains the coefficient standard
deviations, p-values for the non-linear terms, and performance statistics for modeling and
validation (both data-partitioning and k-fold validation). The Phase 2 global model, developed
with data from 392 glasses, is included in Table 5.14 for comparison. The new model consists of
significantly fewer terms (2 fewer linear terms and 4 fewer non-linear terms). However, the
Phase 3 model statistics of R2 = 0.752, R2A = 0.737, R2A = 0.713, and RMSE = 60.187 are worse
than the corresponding Phase 2 statistics. The validation statistic R2V = 0.635 for 83 glasses is
also significantly worse than the k-fold validation R2 of 0.704. The RMSE is considerably larger
than the replicate standard deviation (= 25.73ºC) given in Table 4.15, suggesting that the Phase 3
model has a substantial LOF. This observation has been made previously during the WTP and
Phase 2 model development. Modeling using additional data available during Phase 3 did not
improve the RMSE value. Therefore, all spinel T1% models, including the WTP baseline model
and those developed using the combined WTP/ORP database, are subject to large prediction

 43

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

uncertainties. Figures 5.19 and 5.20 display, respectively, the predicted versus measured plots for
the modeling data subset (359 glasses) and the validation data subset (83 glasses).

 Table 5.15 summarizes the modeling results from fitting the modeling subset of 359
glasses using the Scheffé Cubic model. It contains 22 terms, including only 1 cubic term. The
performance statistics in Table 5.15 are almost identical to those for the PQM model, with a
RMSE value that is also significantly larger than the replicate standard deviation. Thus, addition
of cubic model terms may not offer any improvement or advantages over the PQM model for
spinel T1%. Figures 5.21 and 5.22 display, respectively, the predicted versus measured plots for
the modeling data subset (359 glasses) and the validation data subset (83 glasses) for the Scheffé
Cubic model.

 The Phase 3 Local 1 spinel T1% model was developed using 303 glasses and the resulting
model is given in Table 5.16. Comparison with the Phase 2 Local 1 model, which is also given in
Table 5.16, suggests that both models are very similar in performance. This is not unexpected
since the Phase 2 model was developed for 277 glasses and there is a large overlap between the
two modeling datasets. The Phase 3 model has the same number of linear terms as the Phase 2
model but 4 fewer quadratic terms. Figure 5.23 displays the predicted versus measured plot for
the Phase 3 Local 1 spinel T1% model.

 The number of Group 2 HLW glasses with spinel T1% data is now 139 (Table 5.1). All
139 glasses were used in developing the Phase 3 Local 2 model, which is given in Table 5.17.
Table 5.17 also includes the Phase 2 Local 2 model for comparison. It is seen that the Phase 3
model performs somewhat better, suggesting that addition of new data for Group 2 glasses
continues to benefit the development of local models for the high alumina glasses (the Phase 2
Local 2 model also showed large improvement over the Phase 1 Local 2 model [24]). However,
when compared with the Group 1 glasses (and the number of glasses used in the WTP baseline
modeling), the number of Group 2 glasses is still relatively limited. Figure 5.24 shows the
predicted versus measured plot for the Phase 3 Local 2 spinel T1% model.

 Both of the Phase 3 Local 1 and Local 2 spinel T1% models performed significantly better
than the Phase 3 Global spinel T1% model, suggesting that the use of local models have some
advantages for this response for the present dataset.

5.3 Electrical Conductivity Models

 5.3.1 Electrical Conductivity Dataset

 Sections 2 and 4 describe the ORP-2016 glasses and collection of their EC data. Table
4.1 shows that 73 ORP-2016 glasses have EC data. The total number of HLW glasses available
for EC modeling is now 517 (i.e., all glasses with data at four different temperatures) and there
are 2068 data points. Table 5.1 provides a summary of the number of HLW glasses available for
modeling, including the division of the glasses according to alumina concentrations into Group 1
(361 glasses) and Group 2 (156 glasses). All the high alumina Group 2 glasses were collected
from ORP studies.

 44

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R4220-1, Rev. 0

 The ORP-2016 glasses were divided into a modeling subset and a validation subset. The
modeling subset consists of all the modeling glasses from the data-partitioning validation
approach during development of the WTP baseline models, which included the statistically
designed matrix glasses (HLW02, HLW03, and HLW07) with addition of selected other glasses
to provide better coverage of certain compositional regions [2]. Statistically designed glasses
from Phase 1, Phase 2, and the present phase (HLW13, HLW13A, HLW14, HLW14-UH,
HLW16-UHA, and HLW-MA) were also collected into the present modeling subset, as were
some actively designed glasses that were randomly selected. As a result, there are 453 glasses in
the modeling subset and 64 glasses in the validation subset. Appendix A provides a summary of
the validation glasses.

 5.3.2 Electrical Conductivity Model Development

 Four Phase 1 models were developed for predicting EC of glass melts [23]. They
included a global model that employed all available glasses in the dataset and one Local 1
(≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and two Local 2 (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3) models. The performance of the Phase
1 global model is slightly better than that of the WTP baseline model, even though the Phase 1
global model covers a greatly expanded composition region. It is therefore unlikely that local
models would offer significant improvements and advantages over a global model for EC
prediction. Consequently, only global models were considered in Phase 2 [24].

 The present Phase 3 work also considered only global models and followed the previous
model development approach [2, 24]. However, in addition to k-fold cross validation, data-
partitioning provided a data subset for model validation. The model form considered was a
truncated T2 PQM model on the natural logarithm of the EC. The advantages of using
logarithmic transformations in modeling have been discussed previously [2]. The model form for
the EC model is given by

 The modeling approach was similar to that described above for PCT models development,
where selection of the linear terms was accomplished by comparing the mean response value
(ln(EC) of –1.385 for 453 glasses and 1812 data points) with the 99%-confidence intervals of the
model parameter estimates. The non-linear terms were then evaluated using JMP® RS
methodologies on the modeling dataset. The RS method was used to identify both the significant
cross-product and square terms. Stepwise regression allowed selection of the non-linear terms
based on the p-values calculated from the Student t-tests. The regression continued until all non-

Both data-partitioning validation and k-fold cross validation (k = 5) were performed for the EC
models.

 5.3.3 Phase 3 Electrical Conductivity Model

 Table 5.18 gives the coefficients of the Phase 3 global EC model developed for 453 HLW
glasses. The model is a 26-term truncated T2 linear mixture model. The table also gives the
coefficient standard deviations, p-values for the non-linear terms, and performance statistics for
modeling and validation. The Phase 2 EC model is included in Table 5.18 for comparison. The
Phase 3 model consists of one more linear term and three more non-linear terms than the Phase 2
model. Note that the Phase 3 modeling set comprises many more ultra-high alumina glasses
compared with the Phase 2 modeling set, although both contain about the same number of
glasses (444 for Phase 2 modeling).

 The model goodness-of-fit statistics R2 = 0.941, R2A = 0.940, R2P = 0.939, and RMSE
= 0.161 for the Phase 3 model are similar to or slightly better than those for the Phase 2 model
(both Phase 2 and Phase 3 model statistics are better than the corresponding statistics for the
WTP baseline model). The k-fold validation R2 value of 0.938 is also comparable to the Phase 2
value. Model validation using data-partition, which has a validation R2 value of 0.871, has a
worse performance compared to k-fold validation. This may be attributed to non-representative
partitioning of the validation subset. The RMSE in Table 5.18 is larger than the pooled
uncertainty of ln(EC) given in Table 4.16, suggesting that the Phase 3 model has a significant
LOF. Figures 5.25 and 5.26 compare, respectively, the predicted versus measured EC values for
the 453 modeling glasses and 64 validation glasses. Figure 5.26 verifies the relatively poor
predictive performance for the validation subset, with fairly large scatter of predictions around
the 45º line.

5.4 Viscosity Models

 5.4.1 Viscosity Dataset

 Sections 2 and 4 describe the ORP-2016 glasses and collection of their melt viscosity
data. Table 4.1 shows that 68 ORP-2016 glasses have data on viscosity. Measurements for
another 5 glasses yielded viscosity data at fewer than 4 temperatures for each glass; these glasses
were excluded from the database. The total number of HLW glasses now available for viscosity
modeling is 479 (i.e., all glasses with data at four different temperatures) and there are 1916 data
points. Table 5.1 provides a summary of the number of HLW glasses available for modeling,
including the division of the glasses according to alumina concentration into Group 1 (332
glasses) and Group 2 (147 glasses). All the high alumina Group 2 glasses were collected from
ORP studies.

 The ORP-2016 glasses were divided into a modeling subset and a validation subset. The
modeling subset consists of all the modeling glasses from the data-partitioning validation

 46

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

approach during development of the WTP baseline models, which included the statistically
designed matrix glasses (HLW02, HLW03, and HLW07) with addition of selected other glasses
to provide better coverage of certain compositional regions [2]. Statistically designed glasses
from Phase 1, Phase 2, and the present phase (HLW13, HLW13A, HLW14, HLW14-UH,
HLW16-UHA, and HLW-MA) were also collected into the present modeling subset, as were
some actively designed glasses that were randomly selected. As a result, there are 412 glasses in
the modeling subset and 67 glasses in the validation subset. Appendix A provides a summary of
the validation glasses.

 5.4.2 Viscosity Model Development

 Four Phase 1 models were developed for predicting viscosity of glass melts [23]. They
included a global model that employed all available glasses in the dataset and one Local 1
(≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and two Local 2 (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3) models. The performance of the global
model is very comparable to that of the WTP baseline model, even though the Phase 1 global
model covers a much wider composition region. The performance of the Local 1 model is also
similar to that of the WTP baseline model. The Local 2 models, however, performed
significantly worse than the Local 1 and global models, primarily because of a relative lack of
available data in the high alumina region [23]. It is therefore unlikely that local models would
offer significant improvements and advantages over a global model for EC prediction.
Consequently, only global models were considered in Phase 2 [24].

 The present Phase 3 work also considered only global modes and followed the previous
model development approach [2, 24]. However, in addition to k-fold cross validation, data-
partitioning provided a data subset for model validation. The model form considered was a
truncated T2 PQM model on the natural logarithm of the EC. The advantages of using
logarithmic transformations in modeling have been discussed previously [2]. The model form for
the viscosity model is given by

 q

  q xi

 , (5.14)
                          i 1                 i 1       i  j           i 1 (T / 1000)

where ln(η) denotes the natural logarithm of viscosity (in P), the xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) is the
 q
normalized mass fraction of the q glass oxide such that  xi  1 , ai, aii, aij, and bi are coefficients
 i 1

to be estimated, and T is the temperature (in Kelvin).

 The modeling approach was similar to that described above for PCT models development,
where selection of the linear terms was accomplished by comparing the mean response value
(ln(η) of 4.362 for 412 glasses and 1648 data points) with the 99%-confidence intervals of the
model parameter estimates. The non-linear terms were then evaluated using JMP® RS
methodologies on the dataset. The RS method was used to identify both the significant cross-
product and square terms. Stepwise regression allowed selection of the non-linear terms based on

 47

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

the p-values calculated from the Student t-tests. The regression continued until all non-linear
terms in the model had p-values lower than 0.0001 to minimize the number of model terms. Both
data-partitioning validation and k-fold cross validation (k = 5) were performed for the viscosity
models developed.

 5.4.3 Phase 3 Viscosity Model

 Table 5.19 gives the coefficients of the Phase 3 global viscosity model developed for 412
HLW glasses. The model is a 29-term truncated T2 linear mixture model. The table also gives
the coefficient standard deviations, p-values for the non-linear terms, and performance statistics
for modeling and validation. The Phase 2 viscosity model is included in Table 5.19 for
comparison. The Phase 3 model consists of the same linear terms as the Phase 2 model but one
fewer non-linear term. Note that the Phase 3 modeling set comprises more ultra-high alumina
glasses compared with the Phase 2 modeling set, although both contain about the same number
of glasses (411 for Phase 2 modeling).

 The model goodness-of-fit statistics R2 = 0.979, R2A = 0.979, R2P = 0.978 and RMSE
= 0.197 for the Phase 3 model are almost identical to those for the Phase 2 model (both Phase 2
and Phase 3 model statistics are similar to those for the WTP baseline model). The k-fold
validation R2 value of 0.978 is also similar to that from Phase 2. Model validation using data-
partition, which has a validation R2 of 0.973, shows very good performance and is comparable to
that of k-fold validation. The RMSE in Table 5.19 is larger than the pooled uncertainty of ln(P)
given in Table 4.17, suggesting that the Phase 3 model has a significant LOF. Figures 5.27 and
5.28 compare, respectively, the predicted versus measured viscosity values for the 412 modeling
glasses and 67 validation glasses. It is apparent in both figures that there is a relative paucity of
data in the high viscosity region. Figure 5.28 shows relatively tight scatter around the 45º line,
suggesting very good prediction performance. No under- or over-prediction is apparent in Figure
5.28.

5.5 TCLP-Cd release Model

 5.5.1 TCLP-Cd release Dataset

 Of the ORP-2016 glasses collected and discussed in Sections 2 and 4, 45 HLW16-UHA
and HLW16-MA glasses have data on TCLP-Cd release; the other ORP-2016 glasses either did
not contain CdO or did not undergo TCLP testing. This represents a substantial expansion of the
TCLP Cd-dataset. The total number of combined WTP/ORP HLW glasses available for TCLP
Cd-modeling is now 229. Table 5.1 summarizes the number of HLW glasses available for
modeling, including the division of the glasses according to alumina concentrations into Group 1
(≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and Group 2 (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3).

 Section 4.5 provides a summary of the ORP-2016 TCLP-Cd data collected. As noted
above, the ORP-2016 glasses tended to give higher TCLP-Cd releases when compared to the
existing data and 22 ORP-2016 glasses exceed the (unnormalized) delisting limit of 0.48 mg/L.

 48

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R4220-1, Rev. 0

 5.5.2 TCLP-Cd release Model Development

 The WTP baseline model development work examined various methods in developing,
evaluating, and validating property-composition models [1]. Three different model forms for the
composition dependence of Cd-releases were considered. In addition, in order to support
delisting compliance, two different laboratories (STL-Knoxville and STL-St. Louis) performed
the TCLP testing on different subsets of the glasses, producing results with quite different
uncertainties and an evident bias. It was consequently determined that modeling would be
performed via weighted least squares (WLS) regression based on the different standard
deviations obtained using bias-corrected data.

 During Phase 1 and Phase 2 of model development, TCLP-Cd release modeling did not
consider WLS regression because replicate TCLP testing at VSL had not yet been performed and
comparable data on uncertainties associated with the data set were unavailable. Moreover, the
generalized normalization approach of adding a ln(mCdO) term into the model, which was found
to be beneficial for the WTP TCLP model [1], was not adopted since it did not improve the fit
compared to ordinary normalization of the TCLP-Cd release. Thus, the only model form
considered previously was a PQM model given by

 q
 q q 1 q
 
 ln( rCd )   bi xi  Selected  bii xi2  bij xi x j  , (5.15)
                                        i 1                i 1      i  j         

where ln(rCd) denotes the natural logarithm of normalized TCLP-Cd release in g/l, the xi (i = 1,
 q
2, …, q) are the normalized mass fractions of the q glass oxides such that  xi  1 , bi, bii, are bij
 i 1

are coefficients to be estimated.

 The present TCLP testing included one set of replicates from the HLW16-MA matrix and
Section 4.7 discusses the replicate data. As seen in Table 4.18, the SD and %RSD values
obtained at VSL are more comparable to those from STL-Knoxville testing and are significantly
smaller than those from STL-St. Louis testing. However, since the present testing included only
one replicate pair of glasses and no glasses from any of the earlier (WTP or ORP) studies were
included as replicates, it did not provide a strong basis for determining the uncertainties and any
bias associated with the testing at VSL. It was therefore decided that Phase 3 modeling would
continue to consider the model given in Equation 5.15. Further, only global models were
investigated.

 The Phase 3 modeling approach was identical to that of Phase 2 and the linear terms were
selected by comparing the mean response value (-2.889 for 229 glasses) to the 99%-confidence
intervals of the model parameter estimates. The non-linear terms in the components of the PQM
model were then evaluated using JMP® RS methodologies on the full dataset. The RS method
was used to identify both the important cross-product and square terms. Stepwise regression

 49

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

allowed the selection of significant non-linear terms based on the calculated p-values. The
regression continued until all non-linear terms in the model had p-values of < 0.05. Instead of
data-splitting or data-partitioning, k-fold cross validation was used for the TCLP-Cd release
models developed (k = 5).

 5.5.3 Phase 3 TCLP-Cd release Model

 The Phase 3 global model developed with 229 HLW glasses to predict unnormalized
ln(rcd) values is a 23-term PQM model, with 15 linear and 8 non-linear terms. Table 5.20
presents the model coefficients, their standard deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and
performance statistics for modeling and k-fold validation. Data-partitioning for model validation
was not performed. Table 5.20 also includes the Phase 2 TCLP-Cd model, which was developed
with 184 glasses, for comparison.

 The Phase 3 goodness-of-fit statistics R2 = 0.813, R2A = 0.793, R2P = 0.756 and RMSE
= 0.374 for the Phase 3 model are worse than the corresponding statistics for the Phase 2 model.
The k-fold validation R2 is also considerably worse for the Phase 3 model. Figure 5.29 shows the
predicted versus measured plot for the 229 glasses using the Phase 3 global TCLP-Cd release
model. Note that the Group 2 glasses generally have higher Cd-release values than the Group 1
glasses. There is also more scatter in the data for the Group 2 glasses. This may be an indication
that future work should consider local models as well as global models. Additional replicate data
and data from TCLP tests conducted at VSL on glasses that were previously tested at
STL-Knoxville and STL-St. Louis would be useful for the development of improved estimates of
uncertainties and any bias.

 50

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 SECTION 6.0
 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

 During the present phase (Phase 3) of work to enhance and expand the HLW glass
property-composition models, test data for 137 glasses were collected and incorporated into the
combined WTP/ORP database. The new data include those collected on glasses from two
statistically designed matrices to augment the moderate alumina region (the HLW16-MA matrix
covering the region of 9 wt% < Al2O3 < 12 wt% with 16 glasses) and the ultra-high alumina
region (the HLW16-UHA matrix covering the region of 26 wt% < Al2O3 < 29 wt% with 29
glasses). As summarized in Table 5.1, the total number of HLW glasses available to support
model development range from 229 for TCLP-Cd release to 602 for PCT-Na release. Table 5.1
shows that the addition of ORP glasses over the different phases of testing has significantly
expanded the combined WTP/ORP database. The compiled data include glass compositions,
PCT (B, Li and Na) releases, spinel T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP-Cd releases,
and nepheline formation upon CCC. With the exception of the CCC spinel formation data – the
CCC data will be used to support the further development of nepheline models for Hanford
HLW glasses in a separate task [28] – all collected data were used in the present work to revise
the HLW property-composition models.

 Revision of the HLW PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na models during Phase 3 involved
updating the Phase 2 models using the same PQM model forms. In addition, new model forms to
include cubic terms were investigated for the global PCT models. The modeling results showed
that the Phase 3 PQM global models continue to give goodness-of-fit and k-validation statistics
that are similar to those for the earlier global models, including the WTP baseline models. A
similar conclusion can be drawn for the Local 1 (≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) models, which can be
attributed to the fact that there are considerable overlaps in the modeling data found in the
different phases for that composition region. Validation of the Phase 3 global models using the
data-partition approach, which was not employed during Phase 1 and Phase 2 because of the
desire to include all available data in modeling, performed significantly worse than k-fold cross
validation and the validation statistics R2v were notably worse than the fit statistics R2P. Similar
observations were made in WTP baseline modeling, where the validation statistics from data-
partitioning were worse than those from data-splitting [2]. Non-representative partitioning was in
part responsible for the poor validation performance for the partitioned data. Inclusion of
additional higher order terms in the Phase 3 global Scheffé Cubic models resulted in fit statistics
similar to those for the PQM models. The validation performances of these cubic models with
the partitioned data, however, are all worse than the respective PQM models, especially for
PCT-B and PCT-Li. Therefore, no improvement was realized in adopting the Scheffé Cubic
model over the PQM models.

 With the additional data from more Group 2 (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3) glasses (e.g., 166 for
PCT-B modeling in Phase 3 versus 118 in Phase 2), all of the Phase 3 Local 2 PCT models show
generally better modeling and k-fold validation statistics when compared to the corresponding
Phase 2 models. Similar improvements were seen between the Phase 1 and Phase 2 Local 2

 51

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

models, where availability of additional Group 2 glass data clearly benefited the Phase 2
modeling efforts [24]. In addition, the tendencies for Phase 3 Local 2 models to under-predict in
regions of high PCT releases were not as apparent as in the global and Local 1 models.

 The Phase 3 models developed to predict T1% for HLW glasses also included two global
models (PQM and Scheffé Cubic), a Local 1 model and a Local 2 model. The Phase 3 PQM
global model performed noticeably worse than the Phase 2 global model, with poorer fit statistics
and a larger RMSE. The spinel T1% data have relatively large uncertainties whereas the global
model has a statistically significant LOF. The global Scheffé Cubic model afforded similar fit
statistics, but the validation statistic for data-partitioning was considerably worse than that for the
global PQM model, thus confirming that the Scheffé Cubic model provided no improvement
over the PQM model. As is the case with PCT modeling, the Local 2 T1% model continues to
give better performance when compared to models from earlier phases.

 Only global PQM models were considered for EC and viscosity during Phase 3; no cubic
or local models were investigated. This was based on previous findings from both Phase 1 and
Phase 2, which showed that both the regression and validation statistics were similar to or better
than those for the WTP baseline models, even though they cover much wider compositional
ranges [23, 24]. It was therefore concluded that local models may not offer any significant
improvements or advantages over global models for EC and viscosity. The present results
continue to demonstrate and support that conclusion. In particular, the fit and k-fold validation
statistics for the Phase 3 global models for EC and viscosity are comparable to or slightly better
than those for the respective Phase 2 models. Unlike the cases for PCT releases, the validation
statistics based on data partitioning for the EC and viscosity models are much closer to those
based on k-fold cross validation.

 The Phase 3 TCLP-Cd model performed worse than the Phase 1 and Phase 2 models [23,
24]. In spite of employing more TCLP-Cd release data that provide better coverage in the high
Cd release region, the Phase 3 model has poorer modeling and validation statistics. The
complicated blending effects of HLW glass components on TCLP-Cd release therefore may need
to be investigated using other modeling approaches including the development of local models.

 Results from the present work demonstrate that the development and assessment of
reliable property-composition models benefit greatly from the increased availability of data; this
is expected to continue in the future, especially for local models to predict PCT releases and
spinel T1% in Group 2 glasses and improved models for TCLP-Cd over the wider composition
region that is of current interest. It is also worthwhile to investigate local models that are defined
on the basis of different compositional sub-regions from those previously investigated.
Conversely, the results obtained to date indicate that the development of local models may not be
necessary for EC and viscosity. Addition of new data in those cases will support refining the
global models and their validations. Finally, in order to update and enhance the TCLP-Cd model,
additional data, including data on replicates, need to be collected while new modeling approach
would need to be considered.

 52

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3

 SECTION 7.0
 QUALITY ASSURANCE

 This work was conducted under a quality assurance program compliant with the
applicable criteria of 10 CFR 830.120; Office of Civilian Waste Management DOE/RW-0333P,
Quality Assurance Requirements and Description (QARD), Revision 20; the American Society
of Mechanical Engineers (ASME) NQA-1-2008 including NQA-1a-2009 addenda; and DOE
Order 414.1 D, Quality Assurance. The program is supplemented by a Quality Assurance Project
Plan for ORP work that is conducted at VSL [49]. Test and procedure requirements by which the
testing activities are planned and controlled are also defined in this plan. The program is
supported by VSL standard operating procedures that were used for this work [50].
Requirements of DOE/RW-0333P are applicable to the glass composition and PCT portions of
the HLW work.

 APPENDIX A

 Partitioning of ORP Glasses for Validation of Global Models

 A-1

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models – Phase 3
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R4220-1, Rev. 0

 Appendix A

 Partitioning of the high-level waste (HLW) glass database into modeling and validation
subsets was performed to support the Phase 3 development of property-composition models. In
addition to k-fold cross validation, the data-partitioning approach was used in validating the
Phase 3 global models developed for PCT-B release, PCT-Li release, PCT-Na release, spinel T1%,
EC, and viscosity. This appendix identifies the HLW glasses from ORP studies that constitute
the validation subsets.

 As model development employed all the statistically designed matrix glasses, the
validation subsets include only glasses that were randomly selected from the remaining glasses,
which were mostly actively designed (additional matrix glasses were available from other
statistical designs used to support other ORP tasks such as development of phosphate liquidus
temperature models [27]). The present validation subsets include all of the WTP glasses used in
data-partition validation during development of the baseline models [2]; they are not listed below.
The glasses were randomly selected such that roughly 20% of all available glasses would be used
in validation. However, since the WTP validation subsets for EC and viscosity are substantially
smaller than those for the other properties, the validation glasses in the present subsets for EC
and viscosity contain about only 13% of all glasses.

 In the following table, an entry of 1 indicates that the corresponding glass was included
in the validation subset for that property.

 Table A.1. Partition of ORP Glasses into Modeling Validation Subsets.
